{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-haiku-4-5-20251001","id":"msg_vrtx_01DiPcybGEHP7Py9e9dvWXmH","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_vrtx_012wCUPksYfGXBXpdQzChjQn","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/app/environment/skills/libfuzzer-python-fuzzing.md","content":"# LibFuzzer Python Fuzzing Guide\n\n## Overview\nLibFuzzer is a coverage-guided fuzzing engine designed for testing C/C++ code, but can be adapted for Python through atheris, which is the Python interface for libFuzzer.\n\n## Installation\n```bash\npip install atheris\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Coverage-Guided Fuzzing\n- Atheris uses coverage information to guide the fuzzer toward new code paths\n- The fuzzer mutates inputs to discover new behavior\n- Works with both Python and native code\n\n### Fuzz Driver Structure\nA basic fuzz driver has three components:\n1. **Input generation**: Parse fuzzer input\n2. **Target function calls**: Call functions with generated inputs\n3. **Crash detection**: Atheris handles exception detection\n\n## Basic Fuzz Driver Template\n\n```python\nimport atheris\nimport sys\nfrom your_module import function_to_test\n\n@atheris.instrument_func\ndef test_one(data):\n    \"\"\"Main fuzz function - receives mutated input\"\"\"\n    try:\n        # Parse the input data\n        if len(data) < 1:\n            return\n\n        # Call the function being tested\n        result = function_to_test(data)\n\n    except (TypeError, ValueError, AttributeError):\n        # Expected exceptions - ignore\n        pass\n    except Exception as e:\n        # Log unexpected exceptions\n        print(f\"Caught exception: {e}\")\n\ndef main():\n    atheris.Setup(sys.argv, test_one)\n    atheris.Fuzz()\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n```\n\n## Running the Fuzzer\n\n### Basic execution\n```bash\npython fuzz.py -max_len=1024 -timeout=10\n```\n\n### Common flags\n- `-max_len=N`: Maximum input length\n- `-timeout=N`: Timeout per input in seconds\n- `-max_total_time=N`: Total fuzzing time in seconds\n- `-artifact_prefix=PATH`: Where to save crash artifacts\n- `-seed=N`: Random seed for reproducibility\n\n## Advanced Patterns\n\n### Structured Input Fuzzing\n```python\nimport atheris\nimport sys\n\n@atheris.instrument_func\ndef test_one(data):\n    if len(data) < 4:\n        return\n\n    # Parse input as different types\n    fdp = atheris.FuzzedDataProvider(data)\n\n    # Generate fuzzer inputs\n    string_input = fdp.ConsumeUnicodeString(10)\n    int_input = fdp.ConsumeIntInRange(0, 100)\n    bool_input = fdp.ConsumeBool()\n    bytes_input = fdp.ConsumeBytes(20)\n\n    # Test with generated inputs\n    # ... your test code ...\n```\n\n## Debugging Crashes\n\n1. Save input that causes crash with `-artifact_prefix`\n2. Run fuzzer with `-exact_artifact_path=crash_file` to replay\n3. Check fuzzer logs for stack trace\n\n## Python Module Import Considerations\n\n- Use `@atheris.instrument_func` to instrument functions\n- Consider using `@atheris.instrument_module` for entire modules\n- Import tested modules inside the fuzz driver function when possible\n- Avoid heavy imports at module level in fuzz driver\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":4,"cache_creation_input_tokens":337,"cache_read_input_tokens":18901,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":337,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"5071c4e1-cdba-48de-9792-e8d5a4ff0786","uuid":"ca95e99c-2b22-4ea4-b4da-fc7800a00b0d"}

Different projects use different dependency declaration methods.\n\n## Identifying Dependencies\n\n### Common Configuration Files\n- `requirements.txt`: Simple list of dependencies with versions\n- `setup.py`: Standard Python package setup file (contains `install_requires`)\n- `setup.cfg`: Alternative setup configuration\n- `pyproject.toml`: Modern Python packaging standard (PEP 517/518)\n- `Pipfile`: Pipenv dependency file\n- `poetry.lock`: Poetry lock file\n- `tox.ini`: Testing configuration with dependencies\n\n## Parsing Dependencies\n\n### From requirements.txt\n```bash\ncat requirements.txt | grep -v \"^#\" | grep -v \"^$\"\n```\n\n### From setup.py\n```python\n# Look for install_requires = [...]\n# or extras_require = {...}\n```\n\n### From pyproject.toml\n```toml\n[project]\ndependencies = [\n    \"package>=1.0\",\n]\n\n[project.optional-dependencies]\ndev = [\"pytest\"]\n```\n\n## Virtual Environment Setup\n\n### Using venv (built-in)\n```bash\npython3 -m venv /path/to/.venv\nsource /path/to/.venv/bin/activate\npip install -e .  # Install in development mode\n```\n\n### Using venv on Linux/Mac\n```bash\npython3 -m venv /app/lib_name/.venv\nsource /app/lib_name/.venv/bin/activate\ncd /app/lib_name\npip install -e .\npip install atheris\n```\n\n## Installation Strategies\n\n### Development Installation\n```bash\npip install -e .\n# Installs package in editable mode with all dependencies\n```\n\n### With test dependencies\n```bash\npip install -e \".[dev]\"  # If setup.py/pyproject.toml defines [dev] extras\n```\n\n### With requirements.txt\n```bash\npip install -r requirements.txt\npip install -e .\n```\n\n## Verifying Installation\n\n```bash\npython -c \"import your_module; print(your_module.__version__)\"\n```\n\n## Common Issues\n\n### Missing C Extensions\nSome packages require compilation. Install build tools:\n```bash\napt-get install build-essential python3-dev\n```\n\n### Version Conflicts\nUse `pip freeze` to see installed versions:\n```bash\npip freeze\n```\n\n### Namespace packages\nSome packages use namespace packaging. Ensure `__init__.py` presence.\n\n## Checking for Installability\n\n```bash\n# Try to import the package\npython3 -c \"import package_name\"\n\n# Check setup.py validity\npython3 setup.py --version\n\n# Verify with pip\npip check\n```\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":4,"cache_creation_input_tokens":925,"cache_read_input_tokens":19238,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":925,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":24},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"5071c4e1-cdba-48de-9792-e8d5a4ff0786","uuid":"ae4bb601-0fe8-4541-afb4-4c9b4bd9c916"}

{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-haiku-4-5-20251001","id":"msg_vrtx_01D8kg8cdgmzooTMRG9PysHA","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_vrtx_01A4wMzYuQidXs1tEXr23asC","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/app/environment/skills/code-analysis-for-fuzzing.md","content":"# Code Analysis for Fuzzing\n\n## Overview\nBefore creating effective fuzz drivers, you need to identify the most important and vulnerable functions in a library.\n\n## Analysis Strategy\n\n### Step 1: Identify Library Purpose\nRead the README or documentation to understand what the library does.\n\n### Step 2: Locate Entry Points\nLook for:\n- Main public API functions in `__init__.py`\n- Parse/deserialize functions (JSON, binary formats, text)\n- Encode/decode functions\n- String manipulation functions\n- Mathematical functions with complex logic\n\n### Step 3: Analyze Function Signatures\nFor each important function:\n- Input types and constraints\n- Return type\n- Possible exceptions\n- Dependencies on other functions\n\n### Step 4: Find Complex Logic\nPriority targets for fuzzing:\n1. **Parsing functions**: JSON, YAML, binary formats - often have bugs\n2. **String processing**: Regex, encoding, Unicode handling\n3. **Mathematical operations**: Edge cases, overflow\n4. **Compression/decompression**: Format parsing\n5. **Serialization/deserialization**: State machine complexity\n\n## Typical Important Functions by Library Type\n\n### JSON Library\n- Main parse function (e.g., `json.loads()`, `ujson.loads()`)\n- Serialize function (e.g., `json.dumps()`)\n- Streaming parsers\n\n### Code Formatter/Linter\n- Main format/check function\n- Configuration parsing\n- AST transformation functions\n\n### Data Processing\n- Data type conversion functions\n- Filtering/aggregation functions\n- Format conversion functions\n\n## Documentation Approach\n\nCreate `/app/<lib>/notes_for_testing.txt` with:\n\n```\n# Library: <name>\nPurpose: <what it does>\n\n## Important Functions to Test\n1. <module>.<function> - <brief description>\n   - Input type: <type>\n   - Complexity level: <high/medium/low>\n   - Why important: <reason>\n\n2. <module>.<function> - <brief description>\n   - Input type: <type>\n   - Complexity level: <high/medium/low>\n   - Why important: <reason>\n\n## Potential Bug Areas\n- <area 1>\n- <area 2>\n\n## Testing Strategy\n- <approach 1>\n- <approach 2>\n```\n\n## Example Analysis Process\n\n```bash\n# 1. Check directory structure\nls -la /app/library_name/\n\n# 2. Read main README\ncat /app/library_name/README.md\n\n# 3. Check main module\ncat /app/library_name/library_name/__init__.py\n\n# 4. Identify top-level functions\ngrep -n \"^def \" /app/library_name/library_name/*.py | head -20\n\n# 5. Check for parse/load functions\ngrep -n \"def load\\|def parse\\|def decode\" /app/library_name/library_name/*.py\n\n# 6. Review setup.py for entry points\ncat /app/library_name/setup.py | grep -A 5 \"entry_points\"\n```\n\n## Scoring Functions for Fuzzing Priority\n\nHigher priority if:\n- Takes bytes/string input ✓✓✓\n- Parses structured data ✓✓✓\n- Contains loops or recursion ✓✓\n- Has external dependencies ✓\n- Complex regular expressions ✓\n- Previously had security issues ✓\n- Public API / frequently used ✓\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":4,"cache_creation_input_tokens":758,"cache_read_input_tokens":20163,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":758,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":24},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"5071c4e1-cdba-48de-9792-e8d5a4ff0786","uuid":"1fc64396-f22f-4de2-8277-3be1e29503f7"}

Why important: <reason>\n\n## Potential Bug Areas\n- <area 1>\n- <area 2>\n\n## Testing Strategy\n- <approach 1>\n- <approach 2>\n```\n\n## Example Analysis Process\n\n```bash\n# 1. Check directory structure\nls -la /app/library_name/\n\n# 2. Read main README\ncat /app/library_name/README.md\n\n# 3. Check main module\ncat /app/library_name/library_name/__init__.py\n\n# 4. Identify top-level functions\ngrep -n \"^def \" /app/library_name/library_name/*.py | head -20\n\n# 5. Check for parse/load functions\ngrep -n \"def load\\|def parse\\|def decode\" /app/library_name/library_name/*.py\n\n# 6. Review setup.py for entry points\ncat /app/library_name/setup.py | grep -A 5 \"entry_points\"\n```\n\n## Scoring Functions for Fuzzing Priority\n\nHigher priority if:\n- Takes bytes/string input ✓✓✓\n- Parses structured data ✓✓✓\n- Contains loops or recursion ✓✓\n- Has external dependencies ✓\n- Complex regular expressions ✓\n- Previously had security issues ✓\n- Public API / frequently used ✓\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}

Call the target function\n        result = target_function(input_arg)\n\n        # Optional: validate result if needed\n\n    except (UnicodeDecodeError, ValueError, TypeError):\n        # Expected exceptions during fuzzing\n        return\n    except Exception as e:\n        # It implements and updates the datetime type, plugging gaps in functionality and providing an intelligent module API that supports many common creation scenarios. Simply put, it helps you work with dates and times with fewer imports and a lot less code.\n    32→\n    33→Arrow is named after the `arrow of time <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrow_of_time>`_ and is heavily inspired by `moment.js <https://github.com/moment/moment>`_ and `requests <https://github.com/psf/requests>`_.\n    34→\n    35→Why use Arrow over built-in modules?\n    36→------------------------------------\n    37→\n    38→Python's standard library and some other low-level modules have near-complete date, time and timezone functionality, but don't work very well from a usability perspective:\n    39→\n    40→- Too many modules: datetime, time, calendar, dateutil, pytz and more\n    41→- Too many types: date, time, datetime, tzinfo, timedelta, relativedelta, etc.\n    42→- Timezones and timestamp conversions are verbose and unpleasant\n    43→- Timezone naivety is the norm\n    44→- Gaps in functionality: ISO 8601 parsing, timespans, humanization\n    45→\n    46→Features\n    47→--------\n    48→\n    49→- Fully-implemented, drop-in replacement for datetime\n    50→- Support for Python 3.8+\n    51→- Timezone-aware and UTC by default\n    52→- Super-simple creation options for many common input scenarios\n    53→- ``shift`` method with support for relative offsets, including weeks\n    54→- Format and parse strings automatically\n    55→- Wide support for the `ISO 8601 <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_8601>`_ standard\n    56→- Timezone conversion\n    57→- Support for ``dateutil``, ``pytz``, and ``ZoneInfo`` tzinfo objects\n    58→- Generates time spans, ranges, floors and ceilings for time frames ranging from microsecond to year\n    59→- Humanize dates and times with a growing list of contributed locales\n    60→- Extensible for your own Arrow-derived types\n    61→- Full support for PEP 484-style type hints\n    62→\n    63→Quick Start\n    64→-----------\n    65→\n    66→Installation\n    67→~~~~~~~~~~~~\n    68→\n    69→To install Arrow, use `pip <https://pip.pypa.io/en/stable/quickstart/>`_ or `pipenv <https://docs.pipenv.org>`_:\n    70→\n    71→.. code-block:: console\n    72→\n    73→    $ pip install -U arrow\n    74→\n    75→Example Usage\n    76→~~~~~~~~~~~~~\n    77→\n    78→.. code-block:: python\n    79→\n    80→    >>> import arrow\n    81→    >>> arrow.get('2013-05-11T21:23:58.970460+07:00')\n    82→    <Arrow [2013-05-11T21:23:58.970460+07:00]>\n    83→\n    84→    >>> utc = arrow.utcnow()\n    85→    >>> utc\n    86→    <Arrow [2013-05-11T21:23:58.970460+00:00]>\n    87→\n    88→    >>> utc = utc.shift(hours=-1)\n    89→    >>> utc\n    90→    <Arrow [2013-05-11T20:23:58.970460+00:00]>\n    91→\n    92→    >>> local = utc.to('US/Pacific')\n    93→    >>> local\n    94→    <Arrow [2013-05-11T13:23:58.970460-07:00]>\n    95→\n    96→    >>> local.timestamp()\n    97→    1368303838.970460\n    98→\n    99→    >>> local.format()\n   100→    '2013-05-11 13:23:58 -07:00'\n   101→\n   102→    >>> local.format('YYYY-MM-DD HH:mm:ss ZZ')\n   103→    '2013-05-11 13:23:58 -07:00'\n   104→\n   105→    >>> local.humanize()\n   106→    'an hour ago'\n   107→\n   108→    >>> local.humanize(locale='ko-kr')\n   109→    '한시간 전'\n   110→\n   111→.. end-inclusion-marker-do-not-remove\n   112→\n   113→Documentation\n   114→-------------\n   115→\n   116→For full documentation, It implements and updates the datetime type, plugging gaps in functionality and providing an intelligent module API that supports many common creation scenarios. Simply put, it helps you work with dates and times with fewer imports and a lot less code.\n\nArrow is named after the `arrow of time <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrow_of_time>`_ and is heavily inspired by `moment.js <https://github.com/moment/moment>`_ and `requests <https://github.com/psf/requests>`_.\n\nWhy use Arrow over built-in modules?\n------------------------------------\n\nPython's standard library and some other low-level modules have near-complete date, time and timezone functionality, but don't work very well from a usability perspective:\n\n- Too many modules: datetime, time, calendar, dateutil, pytz and more\n- Too many types: date, time, datetime, tzinfo, timedelta, relativedelta, etc.\n- Timezones and timestamp conversions are verbose and unpleasant\n- Timezone naivety is the norm\n- Gaps in functionality: ISO 8601 parsing, timespans, humanization\n\nFeatures\n--------\n\n- Fully-implemented, drop-in replacement for datetime\n- Support for Python 3.8+\n- Timezone-aware and UTC by default\n- Super-simple creation options for many common input scenarios\n- ``shift`` method with support for relative offsets, including weeks\n- Format and parse strings automatically\n- Wide support for the `ISO 8601 <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_8601>`_ standard\n- Timezone conversion\n- Support for ``dateutil``, ``pytz``, and ``ZoneInfo`` tzinfo objects\n- Generates time spans, ranges, floors and ceilings for time frames ranging from microsecond to year\n- Humanize dates and times with a growing list of contributed locales\n- Extensible for your own Arrow-derived types\n- Full support for PEP 484-style type hints\n\nQuick Start\n-----------\n\nInstallation\n~~~~~~~~~~~~\n\nTo install Arrow, use `pip <https://pip.pypa.io/en/stable/quickstart/>`_ or `pipenv <https://docs.pipenv.org>`_:\n\n.. code-block:: console\n\n    $ pip install -U arrow\n\nExample Usage\n~~~~~~~~~~~~~\n\n.. code-block:: python\n\n    >>> import arrow\n    >>> arrow.get('2013-05-11T21:23:58.970460+07:00')\n    <Arrow [2013-05-11T21:23:58.970460+07:00]>\n\n    >>> utc = arrow.utcnow()\n    >>> utc\n    <Arrow [2013-05-11T21:23:58.970460+00:00]>\n\n    >>> utc = utc.shift(hours=-1)\n    >>> utc\n    <Arrow [2013-05-11T20:23:58.970460+00:00]>\n\n    >>> local = utc.to('US/Pacific')\n    >>> local\n    <Arrow [2013-05-11T13:23:58.970460-07:00]>\n\n    >>> local.timestamp()\n    1368303838.970460\n\n    >>> local.format()\n    '2013-05-11 13:23:58 -07:00'\n\n    >>> local.format('YYYY-MM-DD HH:mm:ss ZZ')\n    '2013-05-11 13:23:58 -07:00'\n\n    >>> local.humanize()\n    'an hour ago'\n\n    >>> local.humanize(locale='ko-kr')\n    '한시간 전'\n\n.. end-inclusion-marker-do-not-remove\n\nDocumentation\n-------------\n\nFor full documentation, please visit `arrow.readthedocs.io <https://arrow.readthedocs.io>`_.\n\nContributing\n------------\n\nContributions are welcome for both code and localizations (adding and updating locales). Begin by gaining familiarity with the Arrow library and its features. Then, jump into contributing:\n\n#. Find an issue or feature to tackle on the `issue tracker <https://github.com/arrow-py/arrow/issues>`_. Issues marked with the `\"good first issue\" label <https://github.com/arrow-py/arrow/issues?q=is%3Aopen+is%3Aissue+label%3A%22good+first+issue%22>`_ may be a great place to start!\n#. Fork `this repository <https://github.com/arrow-py/arrow>`_ on GitHub and begin making changes in a branch.\n#. Add a few tests to ensure that the bug was fixed or the feature works as expected.\n#. Run the entire test suite and linting checks by running one of the following commands: ``tox && tox -e lint,docs`` (if you have `tox <https://tox.readthedocs.io>`_ installed) **OR** ``make build39 && make test && make lint`` (if you do not have Python 3.9 installed, replace ``build39`` with the latest Python version on your system).\n#. Submit a pull request and await feedback 😃.\n\nIf you have any questions along the way, feel free to ask them `here <https://github.com/arrow-py/arrow/discussions>`_.\n\nSupport Arrow\n-------------\n\n`Open Collective <https://opencollective.com/>`_ is an online funding platform that provides tools to raise money and share your finances with full transparency. It is the platform of choice for individuals and companies to make one-time or recurring donations directly to the project. If you are interested in making a financial contribution, please visit the `Arrow collective <https://opencollective.com/arrow>`_.\n","numLines":135,"startLine":1,"totalLines":135}}}

By using it, you agree to cede\n    20→control over minutiae of hand-formatting. In return, _Black_ gives you speed,\n    21→determinism, and freedom from `pycodestyle` nagging about formatting. You will save time\n    22→and mental energy for more important matters.\n    23→\n    24→Blackened code looks the same regardless of the project you're reading. Formatting\n    25→becomes transparent after a while and you can focus on the content instead.\n    26→\n    27→_Black_ makes code review faster by producing the smallest diffs possible.\n    28→\n    29→Try it out now using the [Black Playground](https://black.vercel.app). Watch the\n    30→[PyCon 2019 talk](https://youtu.be/esZLCuWs_2Y) to learn more.\n    31→\n    32→---\n    33→\n    34→**[Read the documentation on ReadTheDocs!](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable)**\n    35→\n    36→---\n    37→\n    38→## Installation and usage\n    39→\n    40→### Installation\n    41→\n    42→_Black_ can be installed by running `pip install black`. It requires Python 3.10+ to\n    43→run. If you want to format Jupyter Notebooks, install with\n    44→`pip install \"black[jupyter]\"`.\n    45→\n    46→### Usage\n    47→\n    48→To get started right away with sensible defaults:\n    49→\n    50→```sh\n    51→black {source_file_or_directory}\n    52→```\n    53→\n    54→You can run _Black_ as a package if running it as a script doesn't work:\n    55→\n    56→```sh\n    57→python -m black {source_file_or_directory}\n    58→```\n    59→\n    60→Further information can be found in our docs:\n    61→\n    62→- [Usage and Configuration](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/usage_and_configuration/index.html)\n    63→\n    64→_Black_ is already [successfully used](https://github.com/psf/black#used-by) by many\n    65→projects, small and big. _Black_ has a comprehensive test suite, with efficient parallel\n    66→tests, and our own auto formatting and parallel Continuous Integration runner. Now that\n    67→we have become stable, you should not expect large formatting changes in the future.\n    68→Stylistic changes will mostly be responses to bug reports and support for new Python\n    69→syntax. For more information please refer to\n    70→[The Black Code Style](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/index.html).\n    71→\n    72→Also, as a safety measure which slows down processing, _Black_ will check that the\n    73→reformatted code still produces a valid AST that is effectively equivalent to the\n    74→original (see the\n    75→[Pragmatism](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/current_style.html#ast-before-and-after-formatting)\n    76→section for details). If you're feeling confident, use `--fast`.\n    77→\n    78→## The _Black_ code style\n    79→\n    80→_Black_ is a PEP 8 compliant opinionated formatter. _Black_ reformats entire files in\n    81→place. Style configuration options are deliberately limited and rarely added. It doesn't\n    82→take previous formatting into account (see\n    83→[Pragmatism](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/current_style.html#pragmatism)\n    84→for exceptions).\n    85→\n    86→Our documentation covers the current _Black_ code style, but planned changes to it are\n    87→also documented. They're both worth taking a look at:\n    88→\n    89→- [The _Black_ Code Style: Current style](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/current_style.html)\n    90→- [The _Black_ Code Style: Future style](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/future_style.html)\n    91→\n    92→Changes to the _Black_ code style are bound by the Stability Policy:\n    93→\n    94→- [The _Black_ Code Style: Stability Policy](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/index.html#stability-policy)\n    95→\n    96→Please refer to this document before submitting an issue. What seems like a bug might be\n    97→intended behaviour.\n    98→\n    99→### Pragmatism\n   100→\n   101→Early versions of _Black_ used to be absolutist in some respects. They took after its\n   102→initial author. This was fine at the time as it made the implementation simpler and\n   103→there were not many users anyway. Not many edge cases were reported. As a mature tool,\n   104→_Black_ does make some exceptions to rules it otherwise holds.\n   105→\n   106→- [The _Black_ code style: Pragmatism](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/current_style.html#pragmatism)\n   107→\n   108→Please refer to this document before submitting an issue just like with the document\n   109→above. What seems like a bug might be intended behaviour.\n   110→\n   111→## Configuration\n   112→\n   113→_Black_ is able to read project-specific default values for its command line options\n   114→from a `pyproject.toml` file. This is especially useful for specifying custom\n   115→`--include` and `--exclude`/`--force-exclude`/`--extend-exclude` patterns for your\n   116→project.\n   117→\n   118→You can find more details in our documentation:\n   119→\n   120→- [The basics: Configuration via a file](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/usage_and_configuration/the_basics.html#configuration-via-a-file)\n   121→\n   122→And if you're looking for more general configuration documentation:\n   123→\n   124→- [Usage and Configuration](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/usage_and_configuration/index.html)\n   125→\n   126→**Pro-tip**: If you're asking yourself \"Do I need to configure anything?\" the answer is\n   127→\"No\". _Black_ is all about sensible defaults. Applying those defaults will have your\n   128→code in compliance with many other _Black_ formatted projects.\n   129→\n   130→## Used by\n   131→\n   132→The following notable open-source projects trust _Black_ with enforcing a consistent\n   133→code style: pytest, tox, Pyramid, Django, Django Channels, Hypothesis, attrs,\n   134→SQLAlchemy, Poetry, PyPA applications (Warehouse, Bandersnatch, Pipenv, virtualenv),\n   135→pandas, Pillow, Twisted, LocalStack, every Datadog Agent Integration, Home Assistant,\n   136→Zulip, Kedro, OpenOA, FLORIS, ORBIT, WOMBAT, and many more.\n   137→\n   138→The following organizations use _Black_: Dropbox, KeepTruckin, Lyft, Mozilla, Quora,\n   139→Duolingo, QuantumBlack, Tesla, Archer Aviation.\n   140→\n   141→Are we missing anyone? Our full documentation is available on `ipython.readthedocs.io\n    28→<https://ipython.readthedocs.io/en/stable/>`_ and contains information on how to install, use, and\n    29→contribute to the project.\n    30→IPython (Interactive Python) is a command shell for interactive computing in multiple programming languages, originally developed for the Python programming language, that offers introspection, rich media, shell syntax, tab completion, and history.\n    31→\n    32→**Python Support**\n    33→\n    34→Starting after IPython 8.16, we progressively transition to `Spec-0000 <https://scientific-python.org/specs/spec-0000/>`_.\n    35→\n    36→IPython requires Python 3.11 or newer.\n    37→\n    38→\n    39→The Notebook, Qt console and a number of other pieces are now parts of *Jupyter*.\n    40→See the `Jupyter installation docs <https://jupyter.readthedocs.io/en/latest/install.html>`__\n    41→if you want to use these.\n    42→\n    43→Main features of IPython\n    44→========================\n    45→Comprehensive object introspection.\n    46→\n    47→Input history, persistent across sessions.\n    48→\n    49→Caching of output results during a session with automatically generated references.\n    50→\n    51→Extensible tab completion, with support by default for completion of python variables and keywords, filenames and function keywords.\n    52→\n    53→Extensible system of ‘magic’ commands for controlling the environment and performing many tasks related to IPython or the operating system.\n    54→\n    55→A rich configuration system with easy switching between different setups (simpler than changing $PYTHONSTARTUP environment variables every time).\n    56→\n    57→Session logging and reloading.\n    58→\n    59→Extensible syntax processing for special purpose situations.\n    60→\n    61→Access to the system shell with user-extensible alias system.\n    62→\n    63→Easily embeddable in other Python programs and GUIs.\n    64→\n    65→Integrated access to the pdb debugger and the Python profiler.\n    66→\n    67→\n    68→Development and Instant running\n    69→===============================\n    70→\n    71→You can find the latest version of the development documentation on `readthedocs\n    72→<https://ipython.readthedocs.io/en/latest/>`_.\n    73→\n    74→You can run IPython from this directory without even installing it system-wide\n    75→by typing at the terminal::\n    76→\n    77→   $ python -m IPython\n    78→\n    79→Or see the `development installation docs\n    80→<https://ipython.readthedocs.io/en/latest/install/install.html#installing-the-development-version>`_\n    81→for the latest revision on read the docs.\n    82→\n    83→Documentation and shell syntax, tab completion, and history.\n\n**Python Support**\n\nStarting after IPython 8.16, we progressively transition to `Spec-0000 introspection.\n\nInput history, persistent across sessions.\n\nCaching of output results during a session with automatically generated references.\n\nExtensible tab completion, with support by default for completion of python variables and keywords, filenames and function keywords.\n\nExtensible system of ‘magic’ commands for controlling the environment and performing many tasks related to IPython or the operating system.\n\nA rich configuration system with easy switching between different setups (simpler than changing $PYTHONSTARTUP environment variables every time).\n\nSession logging and reloading.\n\nExtensible syntax processing for special purpose situations.\n\nAccess to the system shell with user-extensible alias system.\n\nEasily embeddable in other Python programs and GUIs.\n\nIntegrated access to the pdb debugger and the Python profiler.\n\n\nDevelopment and Instant running\n===============================\n\nYou can find the latest version of the development With a compact codebase of **~5,000 lines of Python**, it serves as both a capable inference engine and a transparent reference for researchers and developers.\n\n## ✨ Key Features\n\n- **High Performance**: Achieves state-of-the-art throughput and latency with advanced optimizations.\n- **Lightweight & Readable**: A clean, modular, and fully type-annotated codebase that is easy to understand and modify.\n- **Advanced Optimizations**:\n  - **Radix Cache**: Reuses KV cache for shared prefixes across requests.\n  - **Chunked Prefill**: Reduces peak memory usage for long-context serving.\n  - **Overlap Scheduling**: Hides CPU scheduling overhead with GPU computation.\n  - **Tensor Parallelism**: Scales inference across multiple GPUs.\n  - **Optimized Kernels**: Integrates **FlashAttention** and **FlashInfer** for maximum efficiency.\n  - ...\n\n## 🚀 Quick Start\n\n> **⚠️ Platform Support**: Mini-SGLang currently supports **Linux only** (x86_64 and aarch64). Windows and macOS are not supported due to dependencies on Linux-specific CUDA kernels (`sgl-kernel`, `flashinfer`). We recommend using [WSL2](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/wsl/install) on Windows or Docker for cross-platform compatibility.\n\n### 1. Environment Setup\n\nWe recommend using `uv` for a fast and reliable installation (note that `uv` does not conflict with `conda`).\n\n```bash\n# Create a virtual environment (Python 3.10+ recommended)\nuv venv --python=3.12\nsource .venv/bin/activate\n```\n\n**Prerequisites**: Mini-SGLang relies on CUDA kernels that are JIT-compiled. Ensure you have the **NVIDIA CUDA Toolkit** installed and that its version matches your driver's version. You can check your driver's CUDA capability with `nvidia-smi`.\n\n### 2. Installation\n\nInstall Mini-SGLang directly from the source:\n\n```bash\ngit clone https://github.com/sgl-project/mini-sglang.git\ncd mini-sglang && uv venv --python=3.12 && source .venv/bin/activate\nuv pip install -e .\n```\n\n<details>\n<summary><b>💡 Installing on Windows (WSL2)</b></summary>\n\nSince Mini-SGLang requires Linux-specific dependencies, Windows users should use WSL2:\n\n1. **Install WSL2** (if not already installed):\n   ```powershell\n   # In PowerShell (as Administrator)\n   wsl --install\n   ```\n\n2. **Install CUDA on WSL2**:\n   - Follow [NVIDIA's WSL2 CUDA guide](https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/wsl-user-guide/index.html)\n   - Ensure your Windows GPU drivers support WSL2\n\n3. **Install Mini-SGLang in WSL2**:\n   ```bash\n   # Inside WSL2 terminal\n   git clone https://github.com/sgl-project/mini-sglang.git\n   cd mini-sglang && uv venv --python=3.12 && source .venv/bin/activate\n   uv pip install -e .\n   ```\n\n4. **Access from Windows**: The server will be accessible at `http://localhost:8000` from Windows browsers and applications.\n\n</details>\n\n### 3. Online Serving\n\nLaunch an OpenAI-compatible API server with a single command.\n\n```bash\n# Deploy Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B on a single GPU\npython -m minisgl --model \"Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B\"\n\n# Deploy meta-llama/Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct on 4 GPUs with Tensor Parallelism, on port 30000\npython -m minisgl --model \"meta-llama/Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct\" --tp 4 --port 30000\n```\n\nOnce the server is running, you can send requests using standard tools like `curl` or any OpenAI-compatible client.\n\n### 4. Interactive Shell\n\nChat with your model directly in the terminal by adding the `--shell` flag.\n\n```bash\npython -m minisgl --model \"Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B\" --shell\n```\n\n![shell-example](https://lmsys.org/images/blog/minisgl/shell.png)\n\nYou can also use `/reset` to clear the chat history.\n\n## Benchmark\n\n### Offline inference\n\nSee [bench.py](./benchmark/offline/bench.py) for more details. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_vrtx_01WVkayTmbfoTYBnWWghKo9R","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→# UltraJSON\n     2→\n     3→[![PyPI version](https://img.shields.io/pypi/v/ujson.svg?logo=pypi&logoColor=FFE873)](https://pypi.org/project/ujson)\n     4→[![Supported Python versions](https://img.shields.io/pypi/pyversions/ujson.svg?logo=python&logoColor=FFE873)](https://pypi.org/project/ujson)\n     5→[![PyPI downloads](https://img.shields.io/pypi/dm/ujson.svg)](https://pypistats.org/packages/ujson)\n     6→[![GitHub Actions status](https://github.com/ultrajson/ultrajson/workflows/Test/badge.svg)](https://github.com/ultrajson/ultrajson/actions)\n     7→[![codecov](https://codecov.io/gh/ultrajson/ultrajson/branch/main/graph/badge.svg)](https://codecov.io/gh/ultrajson/ultrajson)\n     8→[![DOI](https://zenodo.org/badge/1418941.svg)](https://zenodo.org/badge/latestdoi/1418941)\n     9→[![Code style: Black](https://img.shields.io/badge/code%20style-Black-000000.svg)](https://github.com/psf/black)\n    10→\n    11→UltraJSON is an ultra fast JSON encoder and decoder written in pure C with bindings for\n    12→Python.\n    13→\n    14→Install with pip:\n    15→\n    16→```sh\n    17→python -m pip install ujson\n    18→```\n    19→\n    20→## Project status\n    21→\n    22→> [!WARNING]\n    23→> UltraJSON's architecture is fundamentally ill-suited to making changes without\n    24→> risk of introducing new security vulnerabilities. As a result, this library\n    25→> has been put into a *maintenance-only* mode. Support for new Python versions\n    26→> will be added and critical bugs and security issues will still be\n    27→> fixed but all other changes will be rejected. Users are encouraged to migrate\n    28→> to [orjson](https://pypi.org/project/orjson/) which is both much faster and\n    29→> less likely to introduce a surprise buffer overflow vulnerability in the\n    30→> future.\n    31→\n    32→## Usage\n    33→\n    34→May be used as a drop in replacement for most other JSON parsers for Python:\n    35→\n    36→```pycon\n    37→>>> import ujson\n    38→>>> ujson.dumps([{\"key\": \"value\"}, 81, True])\n    39→'[{\"key\":\"value\"},81,true]'\n    40→>>> ujson.loads(\"\"\"[{\"key\": \"value\"}, 81, true]\"\"\")\n    41→[{'key': 'value'}, 81, True]\n    42→```\n    43→\n    44→### Encoder options\n    45→\n    46→#### encode_html_chars\n    47→\n    48→Used to enable special encoding of \"unsafe\" HTML characters into safer Unicode\n    49→sequences. Default is `False`:\n    50→\n    51→```pycon\n    52→>>> ujson.dumps(\"<script>John&Doe\", encode_html_chars=True)\n    53→'\"\\\\u003cscript\\\\u003eJohn\\\\u0026Doe\"'\n    54→```\n    55→\n    56→#### ensure_ascii\n    57→\n    58→Limits output to ASCII and escapes all extended characters above 127. Default is `True`.\n    59→If your end format supports UTF-8, setting this option to false is highly recommended to\n    60→save space:\n    61→\n    62→```pycon\n    63→>>> ujson.dumps(\"åäö\")\n    64→'\"\\\\u00e5\\\\u00e4\\\\u00f6\"'\n    65→>>> ujson.dumps(\"åäö\", ensure_ascii=False)\n    66→'\"åäö\"'\n    67→```\n    68→\n    69→#### escape_forward_slashes\n    70→\n    71→Controls whether forward slashes (`/`) are escaped. Default is `True`:\n    72→\n    73→```pycon\n    74→>>> ujson.dumps(\"https://example.com\")\n    75→'\"https:\\\\/\\\\/example.com\"'\n    76→>>> ujson.dumps(\"https://example.com\", escape_forward_slashes=False)\n    77→'\"https://example.com\"'\n    78→```\n    79→\n    80→#### indent\n    81→\n    82→Controls whether indentation (\"pretty output\") is enabled. Default is `0` (disabled):\n    83→\n    84→```pycon\n    85→>>> ujson.dumps({\"foo\": \"bar\"})\n    86→'{\"foo\":\"bar\"}'\n    87→>>> print(ujson.dumps({\"foo\": \"bar\"}, indent=4))\n    88→{\n    89→    \"foo\": \"bar\"\n    90→}\n    91→```\n    92→\n    93→## Benchmarks\n    94→\n    95→*UltraJSON* calls/sec compared to other popular JSON parsers with performance gain\n    96→specified below each.\n    97→\n    98→### Test machine\n    99→\n   100→Linux 5.15.0-1037-azure x86_64 #44-Ubuntu SMP Thu Apr 20 13:19:31 UTC 2023\n   101→\n   102→### Versions\n   103→\n   104→- CPython 3.11.3 (main, Apr  6 2023, 07:55:46) [GCC 11.3.0]\n   105→- ujson        : 5.7.1.dev26\n   106→- orjson       : 3.9.0\n   107→- simplejson   : 3.19.1\n   108→- json         : 2.0.9\n   109→\n   110→| As a result, this library\n> has been put into a *maintenance-only* mode. Support for new Python versions\n> will be added and critical bugs and security issues will still be\n> fixed but all other changes will be rejected. Users are encouraged to migrate\n> to [orjson](https://pypi.org/project/orjson/) which is both much faster and\n> less likely to introduce a surprise buffer overflow vulnerability in the\n> future.\n\n## Usage\n\nMay be used as a drop in replacement for most other JSON parsers for Python:\n\n```pycon\n>>> import ujson\n>>> ujson.dumps([{\"key\": \"value\"}, 81, True])\n'[{\"key\":\"value\"},81,true]'\n>>> ujson.loads(\"\"\"[{\"key\": \"value\"}, 81, true]\"\"\")\n[{'key': 'value'}, 81, True]\n```\n\n### Encoder options\n\n#### encode_html_chars\n\nUsed to enable special encoding of \"unsafe\" HTML characters into safer Unicode\nsequences. Default is `False`:\n\n```pycon\n>>> ujson.dumps(\"<script>John&Doe\", encode_html_chars=True)\n'\"\\\\u003cscript\\\\u003eJohn\\\\u0026Doe\"'\n```\n\n#### ensure_ascii\n\nLimits output to ASCII and escapes all extended characters above 127. Default is `True`.\nIf your end format supports UTF-8, setting this option to false is highly recommended to\nsave space:\n\n```pycon\n>>> ujson.dumps(\"åäö\")\n'\"\\\\u00e5\\\\u00e4\\\\u00f6\"'\n>>> ujson.dumps(\"åäö\", ensure_ascii=False)\n'\"åäö\"'\n```\n\n#### escape_forward_slashes\n\nControls whether forward slashes (`/`) are escaped. Default is `True`:\n\n```pycon\n>>> ujson.dumps(\"https://example.com\")\n'\"https:\\\\/\\\\/example.com\"'\n>>> ujson.dumps(\"https://example.com\", escape_forward_slashes=False)\n'\"https://example.com\"'\n```\n\n#### indent\n\nControls whether indentation (\"pretty output\") is enabled. Default is `0` (disabled):\n\n```pycon\n>>> ujson.dumps({\"foo\": \"bar\"})\n'{\"foo\":\"bar\"}'\n>>> print(ujson.dumps({\"foo\": \"bar\"}, indent=4))\n{\n    \"foo\": \"bar\"\n}\n```\n\n## several\nbuild options are provided in the form of environment variables.\n\n### Debugging symbols\n\n#### UJSON_BUILD_NO_STRIP\n\nBy default, debugging symbols are stripped on Linux platforms.
